AI discoveries in chemistry

187 discoveries from 2020 to 2026

About AI discoveries in chemistry

This selection contains 187 catalog entries dated 17 Jan 2020 to 20 Nov 2026. 187 include structured evidence annotations describing reported tests, the AI contribution and remaining limits. Missing annotations mean those details have not been structured here, not that a study lacks evidence.

Dates follow the catalog publication policy. Coverage is selected and incomplete; entries can overlap or describe several results. Counts do not measure independent discoveries, clinical benefit or worldwide research output. Read the selection and evidence policy or compare catalog coverage by year and field.

Discoveries

20 Nov 2026Maharshi Dayanand University

AI-optimized magnetic nanobiocatalyst enables recyclable citrulline production

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A neural-network-assisted optimization selected a magnetic-nanoparticle formulation of arginine deiminase for citrulline production. The prepared catalyst achieved 70.0% activity recovery, near the predicted 71.18%, and 81.4% immobilization efficiency. It retained approximately 64% activity after 40 days at 4 °C, exceeded 74% after seven reuse cycles, and converted 99.1% of arginine to citrulline. These are laboratory formulation results; model training used only 30 observations and industrial or continuous-flow performance remains untested.

Technology: Hybrid Whale Optimization–artificial neural network (WO-ANN) for immobilization-condition optimization

Editorial relevance 47 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
A feed-forward neural network predicts activity recovery from four formulation variables; Whale Optimization uses the model to select immobilization conditions, which are then tested experimentally.
Test and result
Thirty designed experiments informed WO-ANN optimization; the selected conditions were experimentally tested (70.0% activity recovery vs predicted 71.18%, 81.4% immobilization efficiency). The immobilized enzyme retained about 64% activity after 40 days at 4 °C, was reused for seven cycles and achieved 99.1% L-arginine to L-citrulline conversion.
Limitation / still unknown
Continuous-flow performance, process scale-up and industrial operating outcomes are not established. The ANN was trained on only 30 design observations (21/4/5 train/validation/test); generalization beyond the tested factor ranges remains uncertain.

20 Nov 2026Freie Universität Berlin; Technical University of Denmark

Predicted enzyme structures support shared substrate gates in C-glycosylation

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Analysis of structural models for 56 C-active UGT enzymes found acceptor gates throughout the set and two enzymes lacking a discernible donor gate; AlphaFold3 and source-labeled RobettaFold models supported variable open/closed gate conformations. These predictions extended a gating hypothesis derived from experimental structures and molecular simulations, rather than independently measuring dynamics across all enzymes. For maize ZmUGT708A6, crystallography, biochemical/mutational tests and QM/MM calculations supported a stepwise C-glycosylation mechanism involving water-mediated stabilization of a high-energy sigma complex. The AI contribution concerns comparison of predicted gate architectures; direct mechanistic tests focused on selected enzymes and substrates.

Technology: AlphaFold 3; RobettaFold

Family AlphaFold 3: profile · models page

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original source Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
AlphaFold3 and source-labeled RobettaFold structural models were compared to assess gate architecture/conformational variability across 56 C-active UGTs; experimental structures and simulations established the core gating/mechanistic hypothesis.
Test and result
The predicted gate conformations were compared across 56 UGT models; mechanistic propositions were tested with crystallography, biochemical assays, mutational data, QM/MM and pH/substrate activity experiments.
Limitation / still unknown
Predicted conformations across the 56 enzymes are not direct measurements of their dynamics. Supplementary model-generation details were not assessed here; the reviewed experimental/mechanistic scope centers on selected enzymes and substrates.

18 Nov 2026Affiliation not stated in the reviewed material

Generative discovery of chemical reaction networks

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Version 2 reports NMR characterization of four isolated products, including three predicted diarylation structures; v1 contains earlier family-level MS evidence. The isolated products are supported by 1H/13C NMR; this validates structures, not every proposed reaction path, rate or barrier. The isolated-product result first appears in version 2 (2026-11-18); version 1 reported earlier family-level mass-spectrometry evidence.

Technology: ReactNexus flow-matching transition-state sampler, learned trajectory simulator, and Carina reactive machine-learning interatomic potential (EquiformerV3)

Editorial relevance 67 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Preprint · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
ReactNexus uses a flow-matching transition-state sampler, learned trajectory simulator and Carina reactive MLIP (EquiformerV3) to propose and evaluate reaction paths.
Test and result
Version 2 reports NMR characterization of four isolated products, including three predicted diarylation structures; v1 contains earlier family-level MS evidence.
Limitation / still unknown
The isolated products are supported by 1H/13C NMR; this validates structures, not every proposed reaction path, rate or barrier.

13 Nov 2026IBM Research; Cleveland Clinic; Cleveland Clinic Lerner College of Medicine of Case Western Reserve University

Foundation model and generative AI yield 3CL protease inhibitors confirmed at sub-micromolar IC50

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: An AI-assisted 3CL-protease inhibitor study selected 249 molecules from about five million generated candidates and evaluated 18 using free-energy perturbation. Five molecules were experimentally tested; all five inhibited the target below 1 micromolar IC50, and one inhibited more strongly than its lead. MMELON activity predictions had Pearson R=0.72 in a blind test. This summary is limited to the abstract and November 13, 2026 publication metadata; it does not establish antiviral efficacy in people.

Technology: MMELON biomedical foundation model with generative molecular models

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original source Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Generative models produced candidate molecules; the MMELON foundation model predicted activity and supported downselection.
Test and result
Five synthesized molecules tested for 3CL-protease inhibition after computational candidate generation and filtering.
Limitation / still unknown
Source checking does not establish independent confirmation. Review date: 2026-11-15.

13 Nov 2026Not stated in the reviewed material

FFNN-GA-Assisted Process Modeling and Optimization of Banana Fiber Pretreatment for Retrieval of Cellulose fiber and Other Value-Added Byproducts

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The AI-guided search identified a candidate pretreatment condition that balanced lignin recovery, sugar loss, and cellulose preservation. The paper reports a confirmatory experiment at the predicted condition as well as train/validation/test model checks. This is a small study with data available from the authors on request; the preprint is not peer reviewed.

Technology: A feed-forward neural network modeled measured process responses; a genetic algorithm searched the learned response surface for an optimum.

10 Nov 2026Affiliation not stated in the reviewed arXiv metadata

Composable AI ranks and experimentally tests a new polymerization catalyst

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: CatAI used learned, physics-grounded encoders to rank 7,097 catalyst structures. The researchers synthesized and tested three candidates; the selected CPP-Zr catalyst showed higher polymerization activity than the commercial comparator and retained activity in a 2 L reactor trial. The evidence concerns the reported catalyst, polymerization conditions and assays, not general industrial performance.

Technology: CatAI composable scientific-intelligence framework

Editorial relevance 67 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Preprint · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Learned physics-grounded encoders combine structure–property information to rank candidate catalysts and guide experimental selection.
Test and result
CatAI ranked catalyst candidates and guided synthesis; assays reported activity for CPP-Zr above the commercial benchmark and a 2 L reactor check.
Limitation / still unknown
The tests concern the reported catalyst and experimental conditions; general industrial performance is not established.

9 Nov 2026Affiliation not stated in the reviewed material

Agent-Guided Discovery of Exchange-Correlation Functionals with Physics-based Validation

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Full arXiv Methods/Results describe LLM-agent-guided symbolic search of exchange-correlation functionals with self-consistent physics filters and held-out/external validation; the selected O159 functional retains an 11.7% held-out error improvement over ωB97M-V and comparable external GSCDB137 performance. The result is a computational chemistry functional candidate, not experimental validation.

Technology: LLM-agent-guided symbolic search of density-functional exchange-correlation forms.

Editorial relevance 47 chemistry Original source Suggest a correction

Publication: Preprint · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Frontier LLM agents propose exchange-correlation functional forms evaluated through a GPU fitting and self-consistent physics workflow.
Test and result
Agent-derived O159 was evaluated self-consistently on held-out MGCDB84 and on 36 GSCDB137 datasets; it improved held-out error versus ωB97M-V, while remaining computational rather than experimental validation.
Limitation / still unknown
External generalization, independent replication and downstream real-world impact were not established by the reviewed source.

9 Nov 2026Department of Chemistry, University of California, Berkeley, Berkeley, California, USA

Ab Initio Molecular Dynamics-Guided Machine-Learning Potentials for Mapping Nitric Oxide Interactions with Oxygen-Covered Graphite

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: For NO interacting with 33% bridge-site oxygen-covered graphite, attractive regions narrowed during vertical approach and short-range repulsion decreased upon molecular reorientation. Exploratory AIMD also produced an NO2-forming trajectory after rebound and a reactive encounter; this is a modeled pathway, not experimental observation. Four independently initialized models were compared against an independent 2,000-configuration DFT test set. The selected model had energy and force-magnitude RMSEs of 0.0456 eV and 0.0303 eV/Å. The authors use these results to guide further first-principles sampling.

Technology: Deep-potential machine-learning interatomic potentials trained on ab initio molecular-dynamics configurations were used to relax 300 NO adsorption configurations and construct spatial and angular interaction maps.

Editorial relevance 53 chemistry Original source Suggest a correction

Publication: Preprint · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Deep-potential machine-learning interatomic potentials trained on ab initio molecular-dynamics configurations were used to relax 300 NO adsorption configurations and construct spatial and angular interaction maps.
Test and result
For NO interacting with 33% bridge-site oxygen-covered graphite, attractive regions narrowed during vertical approach and short-range repulsion decreased upon molecular reorientation. Exploratory AIMD also produced an NO2-forming trajectory after rebound and a reactive encounter; this is a modeled pathway, not experimental observation.
Limitation / still unknown
Four independently initialized models were compared against an independent 2,000-configuration DFT test set. The selected model had energy and force-magnitude RMSEs of 0.0456 eV and 0.0303 eV/Å. The authors use these results to guide further first-principles sampling.

7 Nov 2026University of Helsinki; Chemotargets; University of Girona; University of Turku; Åbo Akademi University

Machine learning guides discovery of selective submicromolar CK2 inhibitors

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The accepted-paper abstract reports that docking, CDK-liability filtering, machine learning and error prediction guided more selective CK2 inhibitor leads. The authors report submicromolar hits and a 90% pipeline hit rate; kinase-assay figures support biochemical inhibition. These are discovery-stage compounds, with no demonstrated clinical benefit. The hit rate describes the authors' selected pipeline, not a general drug-discovery success rate.

Technology: Machine learning; prediction-error modeling; molecular docking

Editorial relevance 47 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

AI contribution
ML and error prediction prioritized compounds after docking and off-target filtering.

DeltaCata guides methyltransferase engineering with improved catalytic efficiency in vitro

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The accepted-paper abstract reports that DeltaCata, a geometric graph neural network predicting mutation effects on enzyme kinetics, guided engineering of methyltransferase sGmOMT2. This experimental application goes beyond the model benchmarks; industrial-scale performance is not demonstrated by the reviewed abstract.

5 Nov 2026University of Alicante; University of Almería; University of Salerno; University of Camerino

Neural-potential simulations clarify a phosphonium/HFIP catalyst for CO2 conversion

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Researchers synthesized phosphonium-salt/HFIP catalysts for converting CO2 and epoxides into cyclic carbonates, reporting yields of 86–96% for monosubstituted and 66–88% for disubstituted epoxides at 25 °C and 1 atm CO2. Matlantis PFP v5.0.0, a trained neural interatomic potential, sampled the liquid-mixture structure; radial-distribution analysis supported strong association between HFIP and bromide. The calculated density differed from experiment by about 5%, while NMR and conventional DFT calculations provided complementary structural/mechanistic evidence. AI supported characterization of the new catalytic system; catalyst preparation and performance were experimentally established.

Technology: Matlantis Preferred Potential Framework (PFP) v5.0.0, TeaNet-inspired neural network interatomic potential

AI agent maps proton-gated water loss in perrhenate-catalysed deoxydehydration

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The paper reports a computational mechanism for perrhenate-catalysed deoxydehydration: proton-assisted water loss enables ring closure and alcohol reduction. Under human-defined hypotheses, a Claude Code agent located transition states, which the authors validated. Coupled-cluster-anchored microkinetic modeling predicted a rate increase of about eight orders of magnitude over the best proton-free pathway. At matched conversion conditions, modeled barriers were consistent with the 31.5–34.0 kcal/mol window inferred from previously reported experimental turnover; this was not a new wet-lab demonstration.

Finding: Using learned MACE potentials, simulations estimated excited-state torsional relaxation in unsubstituted biphenyl across nine solvents. Mean times to complete about 63% of the relaxation broadly increased with viscosity in six aprotic solvents, from 0.254 to 3.519 picoseconds. Three protic solvents fell below that trend. Earlier experiments reported a qualitatively similar pattern for a substituted molecule.

Technology: MACE interatomic potential; PULSES multi-agent workflow

Editorial relevance 40 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Preprint · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Agent-trained MACE interatomic potentials supplied the excited-state solute forces for trajectory ensembles of unsubstituted biphenyl.
Test and result
The analysis used 48 trajectories per solvent. Held-out reference geometries gave force-prediction R² of 0.9721 on the excited-state surface. The solvent trend was evaluated from simulated threshold-crossing times.
Limitation / still unknown
The relationship rests on a single-surface simulation and a classical solvent representation. Relaxation times for this specific molecule were not independently measured in the study.

21 Sep 2026Microsoft Research

RetroChimera: predicting small-molecule synthesis

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Published in Nature, the method combines a Transformer model (R-SMILES 2) and a template-based graph model (NeuralLoc) through a learned vote. In blind tests with expert chemists, 90% of complete routes were accepted, compared with 20–50% for the component models or a baseline, and nine of ten difficult targets were solved. Evaluation covered only ten targets; one wrong prediction invalidates an entire route.

Technology: RetroChimera (Transformer and template-based model)

Family RetroChimera: profile · models page

Editorial relevance 53 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
An ensemble of a Transformer (R-SMILES 2) predicting precursors and a graph neural network (NeuralLoc) selecting templates, combined by a learned rank-dependent vote.
Test and result
Blind tests by PhD-level chemists rated individual steps and complete routes for ten difficult targets: 90% of RetroChimera routes were accepted, and nine of ten targets were solved, versus 20-50% for the component models or baseline.
Limitation / still unknown
The route-level evaluation covers only ten targets; one incorrect prediction invalidates a route; NeuralLoc is constrained on reactions outside its template library, and R-SMILES 2 may hallucinate.

11 Jul 2026University of Parma SynCat Lab; University of Parma IMPLab

Bayesian optimization finds mild solvent-free mechanochemical conditions for cyclopropanation and epoxidation

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A Bayesian-optimization-guided mechanochemical study identified conditions for the established Johnson–Corey–Chaykovsky reaction using KOH in place of hazardous NaH, under air-equilibrated conditions and with no solvent or liquid-assisted grinding agent. In the chalcone benchmark, one optimized condition reached cyclopropanation completion in five minutes; the study also demonstrated cyclopropanation and epoxidation across multiple substrates, including yields of 45–75% for four additional substrates.

Technology: Bayesian optimization implemented with BoTorch/PyTorch, including multi-objective optimization of product yield and process mass intensity

Editorial relevance 53 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The BO models selected among six reaction variables (base identity/loading, time, milling frequency, liquid-assisted grinding agent and loading) using experimental yields and process-mass-intensity outcomes. Researchers ran the experiments, performed synthetic substrate-scope tests and used time-resolved in-situ X-ray diffraction to study milling effects.
Test and result
A Bayesian-optimization-guided mechanochemical study identified conditions for the established Johnson–Corey–Chaykovsky reaction using KOH in place of hazardous NaH, under air-equilibrated conditions and with no solvent or liquid-assisted grinding agent.
Limitation / still unknown
The Johnson–Corey–Chaykovsky transformation itself is established chemistry; the finding is a measured, safer and faster mechanochemical process window rather than discovery of a new reaction class. A 5-minute completion time and other yields apply to the tested substrates/conditions; some prior solvent-minimized conditions could not be reproduced. The scale and substrate scope remain those reported in the paper.

20 May 2026Institute for Research in Biomedicine (IRB Barcelona), The Barcelona Institute of Science and Technology; ICREA

AI generates compounds with experimentally validated pancreatic cancer cell selectivity

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The IRB Barcelona team trained cell-line cytotoxicity predictors on data from more than 11,000 compounds assayed across pancreatic-cancer and control cell lines, then integrated the models with a reinforcement-learning generative system to design molecules for cell-selective effects. In follow-up tests, 45 AI-designed molecules or close analogues were assayed; 20 showed the intended cell-specific cytotoxicity, and dose-response testing confirmed 18 of those 20. Across the seven selectivity exercises, at least one hit was found in five; measured IC50 values could be calculated for 12 confirmed hits and ranged from 0.97 to 16.6 μM in the targeted cell lines.

Technology: Cell-line-specific cytotoxicity prediction models coupled to a reinforcement-learning molecular generator (REINVENT-based workflow)

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The authors trained predictive models on phenotypic screening data from >11,000 compounds, then used their scores as rewards/objectives in a generative workflow to propose molecules intended to kill one pancreatic-cancer cell line while sparing another or control cells. Candidate molecules or close purchasable analogues were experimentally tested and dose-response validated.
Test and result
The IRB Barcelona team trained cell-line cytotoxicity predictors on data from more than 11,000 compounds assayed across pancreatic-cancer and control cell lines, then integrated the models with a reinforcement-learning generative system to design molecules for cell-selective effects.
Limitation / still unknown
This is a proof-of-concept hit-discovery study using in-vitro cell-line assays, not evidence of tumor selectivity in patients or an animal efficacy result. Only binary cytotoxicity labels at 10 μM informed the predictors; 45 generated structures or close analogues were tested, with 20 showing desired phenotypes and 18 confirming in dose-response assays. Exact generated structures were not always the synthesized compounds, and measured IC50 spans 0.97–16.6 μM for 12 of the confirmed hits. The paper explicitly characterizes this as an early hit-discovery stage.

Joint molecular modeling identifies seven low-micromolar kinase hits beyond its training chemistry

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A joint molecular model combined bioactivity prediction and molecule reconstruction to estimate how unfamiliar a candidate structure was to its training distribution. In a prospective screen of commercial compounds against PIM1 and CDK1, unfamiliarity-informed selection led to seven compounds with low-micromolar potency and limited similarity to training molecules. The authors also evaluated the metric across 33 experimentally labelled bioactivity datasets.

Technology: Joint molecular model (JMM): semi-supervised molecular property-prediction and reconstruction model using a SMILES encoder; reconstruction loss defines the unfamiliarity score

Editorial relevance 53 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The model used reconstruction-based unfamiliarity alongside predicted bioactivity and prediction uncertainty to rank molecules for a prospective virtual screen. Researchers tested selected commercial compounds against PIM1 and CDK1; seven compounds showed low-micromolar potency. AI supported prioritization and selection, while wet-lab assays established activity.
Test and result
The authors also evaluated the metric across 33 experimentally labelled bioactivity datasets. The reported wet-lab candidate assays directly test the molecular-model predictions within the study.
Limitation / still unknown
The kinase finding is a prospective in-vitro hit-identification result, not evidence of therapeutic efficacy. Seven low-micromolar hits were reported across PIM1 and CDK1; CDK1 was described as independent of the study’s earlier dataset analysis. The study screened only a small selected set per target after computational ranking. It does not establish the unfamiliarity measure as universally superior or demonstrate animal or clinical benefit.

24 Dec 2025Children’s Hospital of Chongqing Medical University; Stanford University School of Medicine; Harvard Medical School and Massachusetts General Hospital; Sichuan University; South-Central Minzu University

PROBY screening uncovers target-selective fluorescent probes for tau, BCL-2 and TDP-43

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The PROBY deep-learning pipeline screened 26,416 target-validated molecules for useful optical properties, identified 11 candidates for testing and found target-responsive probes PE859 (tau), obatoclax (BCL-2) and B3 (TDP-43). The study reports selectivity assays, cell/tissue labeling and ex-vivo tumor imaging with obatoclax. AI-guided structural modification of PE859 yielded 859-2, which enabled in-vivo two-photon and cleared-brain imaging of tau pathology in P301S transgenic mice; its measured tau-aggregate dissociation constant was 54.8 nM.

Technology: PROBY two-stage message-passing neural network pipeline (PROBY-L1 fluorescence classification and PROBY-L2 photophysical-property prediction)

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
PROBY ranked target-validated compounds by predicted fluorescence and seven optical properties, enabling selection and experimental verification of target-specific probes; the team then used AI-predicted structural modification alongside synthesis and bioassays to optimize PE859 into 859-1/859-2.
Test and result
The PROBY deep-learning pipeline screened 26,416 target-validated molecules for useful optical properties, identified 11 candidates for testing and found target-responsive probes PE859 (tau), obatoclax (BCL-2) and B3 (TDP-43).
Limitation / still unknown

19 Dec 2025Worcester Polytechnic Institute; University of California, Riverside; University of Massachusetts Lowell

Diffusion-guided design tests bimetallic ammonia-decomposition catalysts

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A diffusion model guided by a learned nitrogen-adsorption predictor generated bimetallic candidates near the target adsorption energy. Co75Mo25 was among the generated candidates and maps exactly to the tested Co3Mo1 ratio; under the reported supported-catalyst assay it reached 18.6 μmol gmetal⁻¹ s⁻¹ at 520 °C, about twice the paper’s Ru reference under its stated test. Co–Mo catalysts are established prior art, so this entry does not claim the first Co–Mo ammonia-decomposition catalyst or global composition novelty. Ex-situ XRD showed oxidized phases after air exposure and does not establish the working active phase.

23 Sep 2025University of Amsterdam

ML-guided optimization improves photochemical cysteine arylation

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The ChemRxiv v1 reports a two-stage optimization for nickel-catalyzed photochemical cysteine arylation: an embedding-based ranker screens categorical reagent choices, followed by Bayesian optimization of continuous conditions. The journal version reports 88% yield for both optimized conditions; the preprint reports 89% for the shorter second condition. The ranker was initially a weak yield predictor, with better separation after experimental iteration. The study expands a reaction class with earlier precedents; it does not establish invention of cysteine arylation or clinical use.

20 Aug 2025Massachusetts Institute of Technology; Kookmin University

FlowER predicts ten-step pyrazole pathways consistent with known product selectivity

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: FlowER predicted two ten-step pathways to pyrazole products already reported in 2024 patents. Human researchers supplied acidic aqueous conditions omitted from the reaction database. Quantum-chemical calculations of intermediate energies favored the experimentally major product, consistent with the reported 8:2 product ratio; the kinetic interpretation assumes the Bell–Evans–Polanyi principle. This is a computational explanation of existing outcomes, with no new reaction experiment or claim of a previously unknown mechanism. Review covered the February 18, 2025 preprint results and the August 20 journal paper’s abstract, metadata and extended-data captions; the journal main text was not assessed.

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: After high-throughput testing 1,894 compounds and confirming 59 chloride transporters, researchers trained and evaluated machine-learning classifiers, then prioritized DrugBank compounds for wet-lab tests. Five of ten selected drugs—bazedoxifene, centhaquine, brilliant green, tamoxifen and nevanimibe—were classified by the authors as active chloride transporters, with an estimated 50–60% hit rate under their assays.

Technology: Machine-learning binary classifiers and consensus QSAR models trained on experimentally measured chloride-transport activity

Editorial relevance 53 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
After initial experimental discovery data were collected, machine-learning models ranked DrugBank candidates for transport activity. Researchers synthesized no new candidates for the virtual screen; instead, they tested ten prioritized available compounds in liposome chloride-transport assays. Five were classed as active; results extend these compounds' known pharmacology with membrane chloride transport activity.
Test and result
After high-throughput testing 1,894 compounds and confirming 59 chloride transporters, researchers trained and evaluated machine-learning classifiers, then prioritized DrugBank compounds for wet-lab tests.
Limitation / still unknown
The paper combines a large primary high-throughput screen with a later ML-led DrugBank virtual screen; do not attribute all 59 initial hits to ML. The five drug-repurposing hits were measured in artificial liposome assays, not cells, animals or patients. The paper discusses assay artifacts: quinacrine/brilliant green interfere with the fluorescent assay, HCl salts can affect electrode readings, and evidence for brilliant green was weaker than for the other confirmed compounds. Authors report 5/10 active among selected candidates (or 7/12 including two known active compounds), not a generalizable clinical hit rate.

Technology: Multilayer perceptron with genetic-algorithm search over its learned response surface

Editorial relevance 40 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
A learned nonlinear extraction-response model actively supplied the search objective for a newly tested process setting, rather than only fitting previously measured yields.
Test and result
The ANN learned from 17 response-surface-design experiments, with 70/15/15 training/test/validation allocation and a three-input, ten-hidden-node architecture selected through trials. Its learned response served as the genetic-search fitness function. The selected setting predicted 82.45% extraction; follow-up measurements yielded 79.44%, versus 76.68% for the response-surface setting, with source-reported significant difference.
Limitation / still unknown
Seventeen training-design runs produce very small evaluation subsets, and architecture choice by trial/error limits generalization claims. The physical optimized-setting measurement is distinct from the reported high fit correlation; its 79.44% yield is below the 82.45% prediction. The result concerns one byproduct/process system rather than universal extraction efficiency. Gelatin/coacervation optimization, capsule stability and simulated digestion are separate conventional downstream experiments, so they are not credited as additional AI-guided discoveries.

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

24 Sep 2024Pacific Northwest National Laboratory; and Argonne National Laboratory

Solvent mixtures for flow batteries found through an AI–experiment cycle

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: PNNL and Argonne identified solvent mixtures that dissolve three times more than a compound proposed for flow batteries, exploring less than 10% of about 2,000 combinations. The model receives experimental data and guides subsequent experiments; the result concerns solubility, without demonstrating performance of a complete battery.

Technology: Machine learning in a loop with high-throughput experiments

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
A Gaussian process model with an expected-improvement rule chose which solvents to measure; a robotic platform prepared samples and NMR measured solubility; humans selected features and candidate lists.
Test and result
Active learning guided solubility measurements of 2,1,3-benzothiadiazole in binary solvents: 218 measurements from 2,101 candidates found 18 mixtures above 6.20 M; best 6.50 M. On a 98-solvent set, Bayesian optimization needed 17 ± 11 trials versus about 50 ± 27 random.
Limitation / still unknown
Tested one solute, binary solvents only; solubility is not the only property and viscosity, conductivity and stability were not optimized. Model accuracy on 40 test solvents was R2 = 0.63. Real battery performance not reported.

11 Sep 2024Goethe University Frankfurt; Ludwig-Maximilians-Universität München; Fraunhofer Institute for Translational Medicine and Pharmacology

Chemical language models produce experimentally confirmed dual ligands for three target pairs

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A generative chemical language model was fine-tuned on known ligands for paired targets. Prospective synthesis and in-vitro assays found seven of twelve tested designs modulated both intended targets, with confirmed dual activity across FXR/sEH, FXR/THRβ and PPARδ/sEH. Activities varied substantially and were often unbalanced; the strongest reported sEH inhibitor, compound 7, had IC50 17 nM with micromolar PPARδ agonism.

Technology: Target-pair-fine-tuned chemical language model (four-layer LSTM recurrent neural network trained on SMILES, pretrained on 365,000 ChEMBL molecules)

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The pooled fine-tuning strategy combined known ligands of each target pair to bias de-novo SMILES generation toward both ligand classes. Researchers used model sampling frequency, similarity and docking to select molecules for synthesis; assays then tested receptor agonism and soluble epoxide hydrolase inhibition. The model proposed chemical candidates, while biological activity was measured experimentally.
Test and result
Prospective synthesis and in-vitro assays found seven of twelve tested designs modulated both intended targets, with confirmed dual activity across FXR/sEH, FXR/THRβ and PPARδ/sEH.
Limitation / still unknown
This is in-vitro activity of a small set of synthesized molecules, not evidence of therapeutic efficacy or synergistic clinical benefit. Potencies were often unbalanced; several selected candidates were active on only one intended target, and the authors caution the success rate may not generalize beyond target pairs with compatible ligand chemistry. Lead optimization remains necessary.

5 Sep 2024University of Washington Institute for Protein Design; and Department of Biochemistry; Howard Hughes Medical Institute; University of California, Davis; Korea Institute of Science and Technology; Sungkyunkwan University

AI-accelerated virtual screening identifies small-molecule binders to KLHDC2 and Nav1.7

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The OpenVS platform combined target-specific neural-network active learning with RosettaVS physics-based docking to search billion-scale compound libraries. The study reported seven low-micromolar KLHDC2 degron-pocket binders and four Naᵥ1.7 VSD4 binders. KLHDC2 hit C29 competed with a degron peptide at IC50 about 2.9 μM and its predicted pose was supported by a 2.0 Å crystal structure; the strongest Naᵥ1.7 hit, Z8739902234, had IC50 1.3 μM in whole-cell electrophysiology.

Technology: OpenVS active-learning platform with target-specific feed-forward neural-network triage, coupled to RosettaVS physics-based docking and RosettaGenFF-VS scoring

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The target-specific neural network used docking-labeled data to guide which molecules were prioritized for additional expensive docking over iterative multi-billion-compound screens; RosettaVS/RosettaGenFF-VS performed physics-based docking and pose/affinity scoring. The AI-guided screen nominated molecules that were synthesized and experimentally tested. Experiments confirmed KLHDC2 degron displacement and a crystal structure matching the predicted pose, plus Naᵥ1.7 binding/inhibition in patch clamp. The paper attributes discovery to the combined AI-accelerated and physics-based workflow, not to a generative model alone.
Test and result
The study reported seven low-micromolar KLHDC2 degron-pocket binders and four Naᵥ1.7 VSD4 binders.
Limitation / still unknown
Early ligand-hit discovery only: binding/inhibition was measured in biochemical competition, biolayer interferometry and cell electrophysiology assays, not in animals or patients. The paper states further optimization is needed to improve KLHDC2 compounds toward nanomolar potency. KLHDC2 C29’s crystal structure is experimental support for one predicted binding pose; target modulation, therapeutic efficacy and safety were not established. The reported target hit rates apply to manually filtered, synthesized subsets, not the full billion-molecule library.

28 Aug 2024University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign; University of Toronto; Vector Institute; University of Chicago; University of California, Berkeley

Closed-loop learning links donor–acceptor molecules’ photostability to higher triplet states

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A closed-loop synthesis and learning campaign found that solution photostability across a donor–bridge–acceptor molecular series correlated with the high-energy portion of the triplet excited-state manifold, beyond the commonly considered lowest triplet state. Researchers tested the model-derived hypothesis on an experimental set and changed solvent triplet energy to test the proposed Dexter triplet-energy-transfer degradation pathway. The study used Bayesian optimization and interpretable supervised learning to extract the chemical hypothesis from measured photostability data.

Technology: Closed-loop transfer (CLT), combining Gryffin Bayesian optimization with physics-feature supervised learning

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Bayesian optimization recommended molecules for automated modular synthesis and photostability assays; an interpretable supervised model using physics-based features identified a triplet-state hypothesis, which researchers tested with experimental test sets and solvent-energy perturbation. AI guided experimental selection and surfaced a specific chemical relationship; the follow-up measurements tested it.
Test and result
Researchers tested the model-derived hypothesis on an experimental set and changed solvent triplet energy to test the proposed Dexter triplet-energy-transfer degradation pathway.
Limitation / still unknown
The finding concerns solution photostability in the studied donor–bridge–acceptor chemical space; it does not by itself establish a universal law for all organic molecules or performance in processed films/devices. The work used human expertise in the loop and experimentally tested the model-generated hypothesis. Device deployment was not demonstrated.

2 Jul 2024University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill; University of Missouri

FRASE-bot screening identifies a small-molecule ligand that selectively kills CIB1-dependent cancer cells

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: FRASE-bot used a neural-network nativeness score to triage protein–ligand fragments, then used the retained structural features to seed virtual screening for Calcium and Integrin Binding Protein 1 (CIB1). 17 Apr 2024University of Cambridge; US National Institutes of Health, National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases; A*STAR; Indiana University School of Medicine

Iterative machine learning identifies potent inhibitors of alpha-synuclein secondary nucleation

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A structure-based iterative-learning workflow combined prior α-synuclein fibril-binding hits, a junction-tree variational autoencoder, a random-forest regressor and Gaussian-process residual model to rank compounds for repeated testing. Prospective iterations produced multiple molecules inhibiting seeded α-synuclein aggregation in vitro; lead I4.05 had a reported kinetic inhibitory constant (KIC50) of 0.52 μM (95% CI 0.45–0.59 μM), compared with extrapolated values of 18.2 μM for its parent and 36.4 μM for Anle-138b in the authors’ assay. Leads also showed activity in lipid-induced and spontaneous aggregation assays.

Technology: Iterative structure-based learning workflow using a junction-tree variational autoencoder, random-forest regressor and Gaussian-process residual model

Editorial relevance 53 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The machine-learning model ranked a ZINC-derived evaluation set for potency using earlier assay measurements, then researchers tested and fed results from successive rounds back into the model. It proposed compounds for experimental testing; aggregation assays measured outcomes. Initial fibril binders came from an earlier docking-and-screening study, so this paper reports iterative optimization rather than an entirely independent start.
Test and result
A structure-based iterative-learning workflow combined prior α-synuclein fibril-binding hits, a junction-tree variational autoencoder, a random-forest regressor and Gaussian-process residual model to rank compounds for repeated testing.
Limitation / still unknown
Evidence is biochemical/in-vitro inhibition of α-synuclein aggregation, including seeded, lipid-induced and spontaneous assays. The paper does not establish animal efficacy, neuronal protection or clinical benefit. The KIC50 and comparison values are assay-specific; no random-molecule comparator was tested, and model QSAR R² was described as modest. This is related to, but not the same paper/result as, the earlier docking-screen study that supplied starting compounds.

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

9 Feb 2024University of Glasgow

Sequential model-based optimization discovers two previously unreported chemical reactions

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A programmable robotic synthesis platform used inline HPLC, Raman or NMR feedback and closed-loop optimization to explore selected reaction spaces. In a tosMIC campaign, sequential model-based optimization with a Gaussian-process surrogate identified a reaction between toluenesulphonylmethyl isocyanide and benzylidenemalononitrile; subsequent optimization increased yield from the 27% initial condition to 49% (22 percentage points) over 50 reactions. The article also reports a second previously unreported reaction and an exploratory trifluoromethylation campaign that discovered new molecules.

Technology: ChemputationOptimizer, a sensor-integrated reaction engine with sequential model-based optimization (Gaussian-process surrogate); the platform also exposes random search, design-of-experiments, Bayesian-optimization and genetic-algorithm routines

The platform's reported yield gains are specific to these selected reaction spaces and objectives, not general chemistry performance. The tosMIC and phloroglucinol campaigns are reported as previously unreported reactions; product characterization and complete procedure details are in the article/supplement. The system includes several optimization algorithms and the authors note random search can be a strong baseline; the reported tosMIC optimization used SMBO with a Gaussian-process surrogate. No claim is made that the reactions are industrially useful or broadly generalizable. Primary source checked; no independent replication assessed.

13 Feb 2023IBM Research–Almaden; IBM Research–Zurich; ETH Zurich; Arctoris

AI-generated catalyst design led to experimentally tested polymerization catalysts

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A regression-transformer model fine-tuned on historical ring-opening-polymerization data generated candidate catalysts. Researchers selected synthetically accessible, modified surrogates of one model-generated catalyst for laboratory testing. One surrogate, 5c, reached 60% conversion in 32 minutes for monomer 2a and 100% conversion in 45 minutes for monomer 2d, with reported dispersities of 1.06 and 1.34, respectively. The first ChemRxiv v1 (2022-06-27) described computational generation and recovery of a literature-known catalyst. The experimental results appeared in ChemRxiv v2, posted 2023-02-13, and the 2023 peer-reviewed journal article followed on 2023-06-21. The measured tests concern modified surrogates, not the unmodified generated structure.

14 Dec 2022Massachusetts Institute of Technology; collaborating institutions

Machine-learning-guided electrochemical screening isolates five new reaction products

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A machine-learning-guided, chemist-in-the-loop screen predicted electrochemical reactivity for 38,865 potential partners with 1,4-dicyanobenzene (DCB). Automated tests of 20 prioritized, previously untested partners yielded DCB conversion in 16; five produced an isolable major product judged of synthetic or mechanistic interest. The reported examples include a silyl-enol-ether coupling and electrochemical benzylic arylation of benzyltrimethylsilane with DCB, plus three mechanistically unexpected products.

Technology: Machine-learning-guided reaction-competency classifier using a DFT-informed molecular representation

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The model screened a 38,865-member virtual set for reaction competency and, with chemist selection, prioritized 20 new partners for automated electrolysis. The experiments measured DCB conversion, then product analysis isolated and characterized five major products from the selected set.
Test and result
Automated tests of 20 prioritized, previously untested partners yielded DCB conversion in 16; five produced an isolable major product judged of synthetic or mechanistic interest.
Limitation / still unknown
This is an early reaction-discovery demonstration under one fixed DCB electrolysis system, not a general reaction predictor or optimized synthetic platform. Seven of the 16 DCB-converting selected reactions gave complex mixtures without an appreciable single isolated product; five yielded isolable major products, reported at low yields and without method optimization. DCB conversion alone does not establish a product identity, and the three mechanistically ambiguous outcomes require further study.

4 Aug 2022Broad Institute of MIT and Harvard; Harvard University; Massachusetts Institute of Technology; Brigham and Women's Hospital

Machine learning generates active daunomycin-binding synthetic nucleic-acid polymers outside selected sequence families

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The study combined in-vitro selection with a conditional variational autoencoder trained on selection data to generate high-affinity highly functionalized nucleic-acid polymer (HFNAP) sequences. Experimentally generated CVAE sequences bound daunomycin with reported dissociation constants of 9–26 nM despite having no apparent primary-sequence similarity to experimentally selected polymers; competition selections found selected generated variants performed as well as or better than sequences obtained after multiple rounds of high-stringency selection.

Technology: Conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE) trained on in-vitro selection-derived HFNAP sequence/fitness data

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The authors used the CVAE to condition generation of novel HFNAP sequences on predicted daunomycin-binding fitness, then synthesized/tested generated sequences in binding and competition-selection assays. The machine-learning model generated the active sequences; wet-lab selection and competition assays established their binding activity.
Test and result
Experimentally generated CVAE sequences bound daunomycin with reported dissociation constants of 9–26 nM despite having no apparent primary-sequence similarity to experimentally selected polymers; competition selections found selected generated variants performed as well as or better than sequences obtained after multiple rounds of high-stringency selection.
Limitation / still unknown
The finding is target binding by synthetic HFNAPs, not therapeutic efficacy or cellular activity. Binding results are specific to daunomycin and reported assays; the authors describe sequences as having no apparent similarity rather than proving wholly unrelated functional mechanisms. Some competition performance was assessed by in-vitro selection rather than a clinical or organism-level test.

11 Nov 2021University of Glasgow School of Chemistry

Neural-network-guided autonomous experiments reveal an unreported TosMIC reaction

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A reactivity-seeking neural network coupled to an autonomous reaction platform navigated unknown chemical mixtures using real-time analytical data. Among the reactions it identified, follow-up characterization revealed an unreported photochemical reaction and a new reactivity mode for p-toluenesulfonylmethyl isocyanide (TosMIC): six equivalents of TosMIC underwent a multistep single-substrate cascade yielding a trimeric product in 47% unoptimized yield and forming five C–C bonds.

Technology: Reactivity-seeking neural network and junction-tree neural network integrated with an autonomous robotic synthesis and analytics platform

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
A convolutional neural network classified reaction signatures from online NMR/IR/analytical data and a junction-tree neural network encoded reagent structures to help prioritize new experiments. The closed-loop system surfaced reactions; chemists then manually investigated and characterized selected outcomes, including the TosMIC trimer-forming cascade.
Test and result
Among the reactions it identified, follow-up characterization revealed an unreported photochemical reaction and a new reactivity mode for p-toluenesulfonylmethyl isocyanide (TosMIC): six equivalents of TosMIC underwent a multistep single-substrate cascade yielding a trimeric product in 47% unoptimized yield and forming five C–C bonds.
Limitation / still unknown
The source describes several found reactions, some previously known; this entry is limited to the distinct unreported TosMIC reactivity result. The reported 47% yield was unoptimized. The platform identified candidate reactions, while follow-up analyses and manual chemical investigation established the product and mechanism; no broad claim of fully autonomous mechanistic proof is made.

27 Jun 2021ETH Zurich; Thomas Jefferson University; University of Bari; University of Innsbruck; Goethe University Frankfurt; Friedrich Schiller University Jena

Machine-intelligence design yields selective synthetic COX-1 inhibitors from a marine natural-product template

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Using Marinopyrrole A as a template, an automated design workflow generated and synthesized new natural-product-inspired molecules; target-prediction software pointed to cyclooxygenases. In biochemical assays, synthetic mimetics 2 and 2a inhibited COX-1 at 0.10 and 0.16 μM, respectively, and compound 2a was crystallographically observed bound to COX-1. Human platelet assays also showed inhibition.

Technology: DOGS rule-based de novo molecule construction and CATS molecular-similarity ranking, coupled with SPiDER self-organizing-map machine-learning target prediction

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
DOGS algorithmically generated natural-product-inspired structures and suggested synthetic routes; CATS ranked candidates by pharmacophore similarity. SPiDER's cascaded self-organizing-map models predicted molecular target families, including COX. Experimental target assays confirmed the previously unrecognized COX-1 activity of selected synthetic compounds, with crystallographic confirmation for compound 2a.
Test and result
In biochemical assays, synthetic mimetics 2 and 2a inhibited COX-1 at 0.10 and 0.16 μM, respectively, and compound 2a was crystallographically observed bound to COX-1.
Limitation / still unknown
The study tests a small number of designed molecules; automated structure generation was rule-based and similarity-driven rather than a trained generative neural model. The COX-1 effects were biochemical/platelet assays, not clinical effects; the platelet IC50s for 2, 2a, 2b were 9, 13 and 56 nM, but COX-2 inhibition occurred at higher concentrations and selectivity varies by assay. Do not call these approved drugs or imply therapeutic benefit.

11 Jun 2021ETH Zurich

Generative AI and on-chip synthesis produce new LXR agonists

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A recurrent neural network generated de novo LXR agonist designs, and 25 were synthesized in a microfluidic flow system. In vitro screening found 17 hits (68%), and 12 were confirmed as potent LXR agonists after batch resynthesis. Validation is in vitro only, with no in vivo testing, and the sources disagree on the number of designs retained for synthesis.

Technology: LSTM recurrent neural network generator with transfer learning, coupled to on-chip flow synthesis

Editorial relevance 67 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
A generative LSTM pretrained on 656,070 compounds and fine-tuned on 40 known LXR-alpha agonists, with a virtual reaction filter of 17 reaction schemes.
Test and result
25 synthesized designs were screened in reporter gene assays (17 hits); 12 were confirmed as LXR agonists after batch resynthesis, with EC50 0.18 to 4.5 micromolar for LXR-alpha. No in vivo testing was reported.
Limitation / still unknown
Chemical space was limited to one-step reactions, the primary screen used crude mixtures, two compounds were not confirmed, and the authors describe the work as proof-of-concept.

19 Oct 2020Google DeepMind

FermiNet: quantum physics of molecules from first principles

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: FermiNet approximates molecular ground-state electron wavefunctions from first principles. Against the coupled-cluster reference, it recovered 99.8% of the correlation energy beyond a single Slater determinant for the smallest molecules and at least 97% for bicyclobutane (30 electrons). It also modeled stretched nitrogen and hydrogen chains. These are computational results from the original work announced on 19 October 2020. The source page was updated on 22 August 2024 with a separate excited-state study; that later study's carbon-dimer error is not a 2020 result.

Technology: FermiNet

Editorial relevance 60 chemistry Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction

Publication: Publication status unspecified in annotation · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Neural network wavefunction, antisymmetric in its inputs, trained by minimizing energy with Monte Carlo sampling using only nuclear positions.
Test and result
Energies compared with coupled-cluster and variational Monte Carlo reference calculations: 99.8% of the correlation energy beyond a single determinant recovered for the smallest molecules and at least 97% for bicyclobutane.
Limitation / still unknown
The coupled-cluster baseline scales poorly, so results apply to medium-sized molecules; the Monte Carlo method is approximate; excited-state results are within about 0.1 eV of the most demanding calculations rather than matching them.
Evidence sources and annotation context

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

Evidence sources: https://deepmind.google/blog/ferminet-quantum-physics-and-chemistry-from-first-principles/

13 Oct 2020Polish Academy of Sciences; Northwestern University, IBS Center for Soft and Living Matter, and UNIST

Chematica: computational synthesis planning for natural products

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The system plans multistep syntheses of complex natural products, with routes in a Turing-test study largely indistinguishable from human routes; three computer-designed syntheses were carried out in the lab: dauricine, tacamonidine, and lamellodysidine A. Based on over 100,000 rules plus AI, it is not modern generative AI.

Technology: Chematica / Allchemy

Test and result

8 Jul 2020University of Liverpool

A mobile robotic chemist

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A mobile robot ran 688 experiments in eight days in a ten-variable space, guided by a batch Bayesian algorithm, to search for photocatalysts that produce hydrogen from water; the best mixtures were six times more active than the initial formulations. This optimizes a formulation, not a new structure; only the abstract was reviewed.

Technology: Batch Bayesian optimization and mobile robot

Editorial relevance 80 chemistry Original source Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution

Test and result
688 autonomous experiments over eight days in a ten-variable space; the best photocatalyst mixtures were about six times more active than the initial formulations.
Limitation / still unknown
The abstract lists no limitations of this study; it notes that atomistic simulations cannot yet capture multi-scale complexity and that robots are hard to adopt widely across diverse samples and instruments.
